Source verification.
Lemma 10.15 (Carrier–shading commutation rule). Let L be a finite labelled carrier family
and let Y B (ℓ) ⊂ Y (ℓ) satisfy
                               |Y B (ℓ)| ≥ cB |Y (ℓ)| > 0          (ℓ ∈ L)                  (10.32g)
for one cB > 0. Let A be a deterministic operation whose output depends only on the complete
labelled carriers and on carrier-only data (complete containment, dimensions, a fixed label order,
or a partition into finitely many such classes), and not on the location or mass of the shading.
Then applying A before or after the restriction Y 7→ Y B selects exactly the same labels, parents,
and carrier classes. For every class LA selected by A,
                                   X                      X
                                         |Y B (ℓ)| ≥ cB          |Y (ℓ)|.                   (10.32h)
                                  ℓ∈LA                    ℓ∈LA

If a carrier-only partition has at most L classes and a class is chosen only after the restriction
by shaded mass, the chosen class instead retains at least (cB /L)M (Y ); equality of its label set
with a class chosen before the restriction is not asserted.
   There are three noncommutation cautions.
 (C1) A pointwise lower-multiplicity or angular statement remains attached to the shading on
      which it was proved. Mere inclusion does not guarantee transfer; below we transfer it only
      when every counted incidence is kept.
 (C2) For a mass-scored operation, (10.32h) does not identify the outputs before and after
      restriction. Below it is rerun unless its scoring data are separately unchanged, and any
      new pigeonhole loss is recorded.
 (C3) Arbitrary internal deletion from a positive parent–cell graph need not preserve its identities.
      Deleting whole right cells, tags, or positive incidences while keeping every child incidence
      on each surviving edge is the sufficient commutation rule used below.

Proof. The complete label set and every carrier-only relation are unchanged by Y (ℓ) 7→ Y B (ℓ);
determinism therefore gives the same output of A. Summing (10.32g) over a selected class
proves (10.32h). If the class is chosen afterwards, sum (10.32h) over all at most L classes and
take a largest summand. Clause (C1) follows because deleting even one of several incidences
through a point can destroy a lower bound there. Clause (C2) is the distinction between carrier
data and shading scores. For (C3), the edge identity in theorems 4.2 and 5.1 shows that internal
cutting may change the weight or child multiplicity; the saturated rule avoids this failure. □

   The three noncommuting classes in lemma 10.15(C1)–(C3) govern the concrete source order
recorded next in (R1)–(R4).
70                                   ZHIXU HUA, XIUFAN YANG

Lemma 10.16 (Reference objects and permitted reordering). For the fixed source refinement
chain associated with a uniform input, the source and corrected operations may be performed in
the following order:
       Y −→ Ȳ −→ Y sc −→ canonical windows −→ (Y pre , YB ) −→ Y 0 −→ mfine
                                                                                           (10.32f)
          −→ biased label subset −→ (a, b, N )-levels −→ tagged cellular graph.
Source equation numbers refer to the archived version identified in Appendix D. Here Ȳ is
the output of source equation (86), Y sc is the all-discrete-scale output used in (87)–(88), the
canonical windows and their two levels are constructed by lemma 10.3, YB is their good-ball
restriction, and mfine is the global level of lemma 10.2. Every arrow after Y sc is an explicit
induced fine-incidence restriction, a deletion of complete canonical tubelet labels or tags, or a
saturated tagged incidence deletion. In particular:
 (R1) the cap statement is read only on Ȳ , not transferred to a later shading;
 (R2) (87)–(88) are read only on Y sc , whose later local unions are subsets of that fixed union;
 (R3) the good-ball operation precedes mfine , and mfine precedes every biased or dimension subset;
 (R4) every operation after the positive graph is saturated on right cells, deletes complete outer-
      ball tags during one of the two synchronization stages of theorem 10.7, or is the one
      whole-incidence lift of theorem 5.1.
By lemma 10.15, the good-ball deletion commutes with the biased-parent and dimension selections;
lemma 10.1 supplies uniform per-label retention. Every other arrow is used in the displayed
order and on its stated reference object. The product of the retained fractions before the graph is
exactly cpre cbias /(κinc Lm La,b LN ), up to the fixed source comparison constants already named in
those symbols.

Proof. In the v1 order, (86) precedes (87)–(88), which precede the tubelet levels. The canonical
construction (10.7U)–(10.7X) gives (R1)–(R2). By lemmas 10.1 and 10.15, the good-ball deletion
crosses the carrier-only bias and dimension choices while retaining (10.1c); lemma 10.2 then
gives (R3). Saturation of the tagwise positive graph, the whole-tag deletions in theorem 10.7,
and the single lift in theorem 5.1 give (R4). Multiplying the named representation and dyadic
factors yields the stated retained fraction.                                                  □
Lemma 10.17 (The reference cap and the generalized KKT estimate). Assume KKT (β). Under
the thin non-slab alternative, source equation (86) on p. 36 supplies the incident-centred cap
relation (10.35). Assume also the separate coarse-average interface clause (10.35a) in (I2).
Then [GWZ26, Definition 2.2, p. 4; Lemma 3.7, p. 7; Remark 3.6, p. 6] and these two relations
imply (S2)–(S3) on the same reference shading Ȳ . More precisely, (10.35)–(10.37) hold for
every incident-centred direction ball, and every essentially distinct normalized family of density
at least ρη2KT obeys the estimate in (S3). Both conclusions are read on the fixed reference shading
Ȳ .

Proof. Source equation (86) gives, for every x ∈ Ȳ (T0 ),
                           #{T : x ∈ Ȳ (T ), angle(T, T0 ) ≤ ρ} ≍ µ(ρ).                     (10.35)
The additional interface clause (10.35a) states separately that, for every coarse label S,

                                      µ T[S], Ȳ |T[S] ≍ µ(ρ).                             (10.35a)

                                        Nρ |Tρ | ≲log |T|.                             (10.36)

                  T ∈T[S] |Ȳ (T )|. Bounded representation and uniform branching give
                P

                  11. Sharpness of the rectangular density exponent
   The rectangular theorem converts the planar Furstenberg incidence exponent of Dąbrowski–
Orponen–Villa [DOV22] into the shading power q = 2 − σ. The construction below shows that
this power is optimal when one asks for a bound uniform over every allowed aspect ratio. This
is a uniform statement: a fixed parent shape may admit a stronger dependence on the shading
density.
Proposition 11.1 (Uniform rectangular sharpness). For every fixed 0 < σ ≤ 1, the exponent
2 − σ cannot be replaced, uniformly over δ ≤ a ≤ b ≤ r, by a smaller exponent of the shading
density. Precisely, if q ′ < 2 − σ and 0 < ε < 2 − σ − q ′ , there do not exist constants c > 0 and
δ0 > 0 such that every family satisfying (6.3) with a uniformly bounded constant C0 , at every
0 < δ < δ0 , obeys
                                 |B ∩ Na (U (TB , Y ))|              ′
                                                        ≥ c(a/b)ε λqB .                       (11.0)
                                         |B|
The constants in this negation may depend on the fixed σ, q ′ , ε, but not on δ, a, b, r.
Lemma 11.2 (Two-line convex-hull volume). For p = (p0 , p1 ) ∈ [0, 1]2 , let
                              ℓp = {(x, (1 − x)p0 + xp1 ) : 0 ≤ x ≤ 1}.
If ℓp (cδ) denotes its cδ-neighbourhood in a fixed bounded rectangle, then
                                                              ≳ δ + |p − p′ |.
                                                          
                               conv ℓp (cδ) ∪ ℓp′ (cδ)                                        (11.1a)

Proof. At the vertical slice x, the convex hull contains the interval between the two affine graph
values, enlarged by cδ. Hence its area is at least a fixed multiple of
                                  Z 1

                                    0

                                         R

possible zero. The last display is therefore bounded below by c(δ + |p − p′ |).                   □

Proof of proposition 11.1. Planar extremal data. Put t = 1 + σ and fix u ∈ [0, 1]. Proposition 5.2
of [DOV22] gives a δ-separated (δ, t, C)-set P ⊂ [0, 1]2 , with |P | ≍ δ −t , and a cδ-separated
bounded-chart line family Lu,t such that every p ∈ P is δ-incident to at least δ −u lines and

                                         |Lu,t | ≲ δ −2u−(1−u)(t−1) .                         (11.1b)
Apply bounded point–line duality. A source line becomes a δ-separated point in a fixed parameter
rectangle, while a source point p = (p0 , p1 ) becomes, up to one fixed affine change of coordinates,
the graph segment ℓp in lemma 11.2. Incidence means that the dual point lies in a fixed
Cδ-neighbourhood of that graph.

The δ-neighbourhood of E only replaces each dual δ-square and the transverse interval by fixed
dilates. To make this step literal, let qℓ be the dual parameter point of ℓ ∈ Lu,t . The points
qℓ are cδ-separated in a fixed parameter rectangle, so the fixed dilates C ′ Q(qℓ , δ) have overlap
bounded by a constant depending only on C ′ . Every square occurring after the neighbourhood
operation is contained in one such C ′ Q(qℓ , δ); no new parameter point is created. Hence

                                              C ′ Q(qℓ , δ) ≤ Cδ 2 |Lu,t |,
                                       [

                                     ℓ∈Lu,t

and (11.1d) still controls its volume up to an absolute constant. Equations (11.1c)–(11.1d) give
λB ≍ λ and
                                       |B ∩ Na (E)|
                                                    ≲ λ2−σ .                              (11.1)
                                           |B|
  It remains to verify the hypothesis for every convex K ⊂ B. Put PK = {p : Tp ⊂ K}. If
PK ̸= ∅, convexity and the common transverse δ-interval imply
                                    |K|
                                        ≳ δ + diam(PK )                          (11.1e)
                                    |B|
by applying lemma 11.2 to a diametral pair (and by the volume of one tube when PK is a

                                                                            1+σ
                              #PK                                     |K|

                                  ≲ (δ + diam(PK ))t ≲                             .        (11.1f)

This is exactly the all-convex complete-child condition (6.3), uniformly in K, rather than only a
parameter-ball estimate.
  Finally let q ′ < 2 − σ, choose 0 < ε < 2 − σ − q ′ , take u = 0, and let δ ↓ 0. Then λ = δ, so
                              ′
a uniform lower bound δ ε λq is asymptotically larger than the upper bound λ2−σ in (11.1), a
contradiction. Varying u gives the whole resolution range δ ≤ λ ≤ 1, up to harmless integer
rounding. The proof includes σ = 1; constants are allowed to depend on the fixed σ.            □
                            CELLULAR MAXIMAL-DENSITY FACTORING                                  79

   Uniform sharpness does not imply aspectwise sharpness. If a = b ≪ r, one shaded tube
already gives
                                |B ∩ Na (Y (T ))| ≳ λ|B|.                         (11.2)
Indeed, the longitudinal projection of Y (T ) has measure ≳ λr, and each selected slice fills a
fixed portion of the a × a parent cross-section after a-thickening. Conversely, consider the model
in which a sufficiently dense transverse family fills the parent cross-section over one common
longitudinal interval I of length |I| = λr, and take its shading to be exactly that filled portion.
Fubini’s theorem, with the two endpoint layers created by a-thickening, gives
                                     |B ∩ Na (E)|
                                                  ≍ λ + a/r.                                (11.3)
                                         |B|
This is only an aspect-ratio model calculation, not another family satisfying all hypotheses
of proposition 11.1. It explains why density power one is the natural target above the a/r
resolution plateau in the square cross-section regime. At a ≍ r, a single a-ball already occupies
a parent-scale volume.
   Several natural degenerations do not improve (11.1). A fixed union of translated bushes
changes only constants. Sparse multi-bushes produce a planar union of size λ/N + λ2 , never
smaller than the quadratic obstruction in their valid range. Parallel stacks have relative union
size λ + δ. Exact projection fibres are charged either by the all-convex constant or by the atom
weight in the weighted planar argument. Sharp wedges expose a boundary-localization problem,
but the elementary hidden-ray constructions violate the absolute all-convex hypothesis before
they violate the desired conclusion.
Remark 11.3. The DOV construction covers a = δ, b = r, the endpoints λ = δ, 1, and σ = 1. It
does not prove aspectwise optimality for b = r, a ≫ δ, nor does it describe λ < δ, where the
δ-neighbourhood has a resolution plateau. Constants are not asserted uniform as σ ↓ 0.

             12. Conclusion: stability and the sharp-convex boundary
   The method rests on a stable unit of refinement: a positive incidence between a labelled
permanent parent and a half-open cell at the shortest parent scale. Selecting these incidences
as whole units lets one edge set control child mass, parent mass, both multiplicities, angular
selection, and the final lift. An arbitrary refinement inside a cell could instead retain parent
mass where no child mass remains. The fixed sequence of whole-incidence lifts has an explicit
product of retention fractions, and labelled counting retains the distinct child partitions and
multiplicity contributions of coincident parents.
   This mechanism yields the unconditional cellular theorem, the two Section 6 applications, and
the admissible very-non-sticky closure. The weighted planar input is the layer-cake consequence
of [DOV22], the quadratic endpoint is the Córdoba mechanism [Cór77], and the terminal gain
is the one from [GWZ26]. Assuming both KKT (β) and KF (β), and under the explicit interface
(I1)–(I4), proposition 10.19 and corollary 10.21 construct the admissible data and supply the
corresponding conditional input to the reduction toward Main Lemma 2.
   The principal geometric boundary is the sharp-convex-parent analogue of the rectangular
theorem. A convex parent W with a sharp boundary can be placed inside the comparable-volume
source Löwner box BW , and complete-child counts transfer monotonically to the box. The
induced shading does not. For a triangular prism, a box neighbourhood may extend substantially
beyond the slanted boundary while the numerator is clipped to the prism. The complete-child
hypothesis counts tubes contained in a convex test body, while a quantitative comparison of
the two neighbourhood measures would require additional control of the induced shading. This
boundary interface is where the Löwner-box reduction stops.
80                                            ZHIXU HUA, XIUFAN YANG

   The elementary wedge models considered here do not disprove the sharp-convex statement:
concentrating a fraction of the tube family in a subwedge worsens the hypothesis constant by a
reciprocal power, stronger than the direct boundary loss. The remaining question is whether
one can replace the permanent rectangle by an equally stable unit inside W , perhaps through
boundary-sensitive cellularization, a weighted coarea argument that retains the transverse factor,
or a hypothesis controlling induced shadings rather than complete containment alone.

Lemma A.4 (Capped graph-tube intersection). Let Tp , Tp′ be two constant-width h-tubes whose
carriers are graphs over the same fixed bounded interval and whose slope–intercept parameters
lie in one fixed bounded chart. Then
                                                            h2
                                      |Tp ∩ Tp′ | ≲                   .                         (A.9)
                                                        h + |p − p′ |
The implicit constant is uniform over the chart, and the estimate includes coincident parameters.
                                   CELLULAR MAXIMAL-DENSITY FACTORING                                   83

Proof. Write γ for the slope separation and d for the intercept separation. If γ ≥ h and the
two carrier graphs cross in a fixed enlargement of the graph interval, slicing parallel to the first
carrier gives intersection area O(h2 /γ). If they do not cross there, the intercept gap gives the
stronger bound O(h2 /d), with empty intersection once the gap exceeds the fixed enlargement. In
this crossing regime the parameter distance is comparable, up to chart constants, to the larger
of γ and the corresponding crossing-scale separation. If γ < h, either the tubes are disjoint
or their intersection has area O(h); moreover a nonempty intersection forces d = O(h), hence
|p − p′ | = O(h). Thus every case is bounded by the right-hand side of (A.9). When p = p′ , it
reduces to the trivial O(h) tube-area bound.                                                      □

                             Appendix B. A labelled slab L2 estimate
  The broad-middle-axis branch needs an L2 estimate that counts repeated geometric slabs

                      Appendix C. A labelled-multiset filling lemma
   The transverse branch requires a filling statement on a labelled multiset, so the argument
is formulated on the index set and never identifies coincident carriers. Boundary cells are the
only delicate point. We admit only cells containing a full cα × cρ × cρ piece, and all densities
are computed with the resulting true geometric denominator. We do not assert the stronger
thickened-proxy conclusions of [GWZ26, Lemma 6.13, p. 18], which are not needed for the
transverse branch.
   Throughout this appendix a rectangular α × ρ × 1 plank means a labelled box
                P = xP + {ts es + tm em + tℓ eℓ : |ts | ≤ α/2, |tm | ≤ ρ/2, |tℓ | ≤ 1/2},     (C.0)
86                                   ZHIXU HUA, XIUFAN YANG

with a fixed ordered orthonormal frame (es , em , eℓ ). Its two-long-axis plane is span{em , eℓ }.
The frame is part of the label even when side lengths agree. For Cbox ≥ 1, a Cbox -comparable
rectangular α × ρ × 1 plank has the same form, with ordered side lengths aP ≤ bP ≤ ℓP satisfying
             −1                          −1                         −1
            Cbox α ≤ aP ≤ Cbox α,       Cbox ρ ≤ bP ≤ Cbox ρ,      Cbox ≤ ℓP ≤ Cbox .        (C.0′ )
The exact class (C.0) is the case Cbox = 1. Thus the full-piece claims below concern rectangular
boxes with controlled side lengths, not a general convex body specified only by Löwner dimensions.
Lemma C.1 (Full-piece translated grids for rectangular planks). Fix Cbox ≥ 1. Let S be a
θ × 1 × 1 slab and let P be a Cbox -comparable rectangular α × ρ × 1 plank in the sense of (C.0′ ),
whose two-long-axis plane makes angle O(θ) with the plane of S. Suppose θρ ≥ 4α. There is a
structural finite family of translated, half-open grids, in coordinates adapted to S, whose cells
have dimensions comparable to
                                             θρ × ρ × ρ,                                    (C.1)
with the following property. For every x ∈ P , at least one grid has a cell Q = Q(x) for which
                                     cαρ2 ≤ |P ∩ Q| ≤ Cαρ2 ,                                  (C.2)
and P ∩ Q contains a cα × cρ × cρ subplank having x as one of its points (possibly on its
boundary). The constants and the number of grids are independent of α, ρ, θ, P, S, with structural
dependence on Cbox . For each fixed grid G, the set
            Ifull (P, G) := {x ∈ P : QG (x) has the displayed full-piece property at x}
is measurable; here QG (x) is the unique half-open cell selected by the fixed boundary order.
Proof. Straighten the middle plane of S. In its two tangential coordinates use mesh C0 ρ, and in
the normal coordinate use mesh C0 θρ, where C0 is a sufficiently large structural constant. Take
three equally spaced translates in each coordinate. For every point one translate places it a fixed
proportion of the mesh away from all cell faces. Inside a tangential disk of radius cρ, the middle
plane of P drifts by at most Cθρ in the normal coordinate. Choosing c small relative to C0 , and
using α ≤ θρ/4, leaves a cα × cρ × cρ subplank in the cell. If x lies on, or within cρ of, a middle
or long endpoint face of the rectangular carrier, choose the corresponding cρ-interval one-sided
toward the interior, shortening on the endpoint side and extending by the same amount on the
                                                                −1
interior side. Since both tangential side lengths are at least Cbox ρ, choosing c below a structural
                −1
multiple of Cbox gives these intervals even at a corner. In the short coordinate use a one-sided
interval of length cα containing x; this fits because the actual short side has length at least
  −1
Cbox   α. Hence the lower volume in (C.2) is uniform at all faces, edges, and corners. Conversely,
the projection of P ∩ Q to the middle plane of P has area OCbox (ρ2 ); Fubini across its OCbox (α)
short thickness gives the upper bound in (C.2). Half-open cell faces and a fixed order of all
endpoint faces assign all boundary points deterministically. For a fixed grid, membership in
Ifull (P, G) is described by finitely many weak and strict affine inequalities in the coordinates
of x: the half-open inequalities select QG (x), and the remaining inequalities assert that the
prescribed one-sided intervals fit in both P and that cell. It is therefore a finite union of Borel
polyhedral pieces, hence measurable.                                                              □
Lemma C.2 (Bounded packing of slab parameters). Work in a fixed bounded ball. For 0 < θ ≤ 1,
there is a finite half-open parameter net Sθ of θ × 1 × 1 slabs with the following two properties.
Every such slab is contained in a fixed enlargement of some S ∈ Sθ whose middle plane is at
projective-plane distance at most Cθ. Conversely, for every point x and every plane cap C of
radius C0 θ,
                             #{S ∈ Sθ : x ∈ CS, PS ∈ C} ≤ C(C0 , C).                       (C.2a)
The same estimate holds for labelled candidates after first grouping equal net parameters; labels
within one group are not discarded.
                              CELLULAR MAXIMAL-DENSITY FACTORING                                    87

Proof. Parameterize an unoriented affine plane by (n, t) ∈ RP2 × [−C1 , C1 ], where its equation
is n · y = t. Take a cθ-separated net in RP2 and, for every net normal, a half-open cθ-mesh in t.
Associate to a parameter pair the portion, in the fixed ambient ball, of the Cθ-neighbourhood
of that plane. Nearest-net assignment, with the half-open order on Voronoi cells and on the
offset intervals, gives the covering assertion.
   If PS ∈ C, separation and two-dimensional packing on RP2 leave OC0 ,C (1) possible normals.
For one normal, the condition x ∈ CS restricts t to an interval of length OC (θ), which contains
only OC (1) offset-net points. Their product proves (C.2a). Grouping labels by their net
parameter changes neither the physical union nor this count of geometric parameter groups. □

is ϕ ∈ (0, π/2]. Then

Proof. The first, trivial bound follows by enclosing Q in a ball of radius CQ ρ and slicing NCP α (H1 )
normal to H1 . For the transverse bound choose unit normals ni , write Hi = {x : ni · x = ti },
and put τ = (n1 × n2 )/|n1 × n2 |. The affine map
                             x 7−→ (n1 · x − t1 , n2 · x − t2 , τ · (x − xQ ))                 (C.2h)
has Jacobian sin ϕ, where xQ ∈ Q is arbitrary. On P1 ∩ P2 ∩ Q, the first two coordinates range
over intervals of length O(α), and the last over an interval of length O(ρ). The change-of-
variables formula gives Cα2 ρ/ sin ϕ. Since sin ϕ ≥ (2/π)ϕ on [0, π/2], (C.2f) follows. Under the
two assumptions in (C.2g), the transverse bound is at most CBang α2 ρ/θ, and the buffer makes
this at most the trivial scale Cαρ2 . Changing an offset translates a coordinate interval, while
caps or cell faces only remove points.                                                         □

Proof. Write the multiset as a finite index set I, a carrier map i 7→ Pi , and shadings Yi ⊂ Pi .
All planks have volume comparable to αρ, and all sums below run over indices.
   Choose the half-open slab net of lemma C.2. Assign each Pi to the first slab S(i) whose
fixed enlargement contains it and whose middle plane is O(θ)-parallel to that of Pi . At any
shaded point, the upper angular-diameter hypothesis and (C.2a) show that the incident labels
belong to only C1 slab-parameter groups. Coincident labels remain together inside their group
and therefore do not invalidate the geometric packing count. Assign the point, with a fixed tie
rule, to a group having maximal incident labelled multiplicity, and retain at that point only the
labels in this group. This pointwise restriction retains at least C1−1 M (Y ), and its multiplicity is
between m/C1 and 2m.
   For each group use lemma C.1. Averaging over the structural finite set of grids, choose one
translated grid per group for which the labelled mass of points lying in a full piece is at least a
fixed fraction of the group mass. Write this structural fraction as g0 > 0, and restrict to those
points. Let E0 be the set on which the retained grid multiplicity is below c1 m. Before this
restriction the multiplicity was at least m/C1 , so the discarded incidences contribute at least
(C1−1 − c1 )m|E0 |, whereas the retained mass on E0 is below c1 m|E0 |. Since the total discarded
mass is at most the pre-grid mass,

All choices were made on the label index set, so coincident carriers were counted with their
full multiplicity. Label deletion can only decrease labelled ∆max . The fixed fractions retained
at the grouping, grid, multiplicity, density-level, and dense-subcell steps have a fixed product
cfill > 0. With ωfill (α) = log(1/cfill )/ log(1/α), this product is αωfill (α) , so the final refinement
satisfies (C.2d′ ) with the displayed uniform modulus. This proves the theorem.                        □

    Appendix D. Quantitative constants, source map, and version convention
    This appendix gathers the quantitative hierarchy, the exact interfaces with the cited sources,
and the archival convention used for those comparisons. Keeping these records together separates
provenance from the mathematical narrative while fixing the versions on which every source-
dependent statement is based.
Version convention and reproducibility. The frozen source identifier for this release line is
CMDF-R7-20260827. The manuscript is self-contained at the TeX level: the bibliography is
embedded, and three clean pdflatex runs resolve the contents and cross-references. The external
files checked for the source-dependent comparisons have the following fixed records.

These archival identifiers are intentionally separate from the conventional bibliography.
   All source-specific comparisons in the paper use these dated versions. In particular, “the
archived GWZ manuscript” means arXiv:2601.14411v1 (20 January 2026). The symbol ⪆ is
reserved for a literal source quotation and is not a third asymptotic convention in the proofs.
The GWZ phrase “outer John ellipsoid” denotes the minimum-volume containing Löwner
ellipsoid used in lemma 8.3, rather than the maximum-volume inscribed John ellipsoid; the
repeated-eigenvalue tie rule in this paper fixes the labelled frame and is not attributed to the
source.
Section 6 version comparison. Branching comparability is used in (6.6e)–(6.6f), (9.1b′ )–(9.1b′′ ),
and (9.5), and the arguments allow highly concentrated fine descendants. The pinned v1 proof of
Proposition 6.6 prints three negative density exponents incompatible with densities bounded by
one and an unintroduced left member in equation (49). The present derivation instead obtains
(9.2d)–(9.2e) from Lemma 6.4 through proposition 9.5 and (9.5c)–(9.5d) from the labelled
Lemma 6.1 argument at (9.1c). These documentary discrepancies therefore do not enter the
logical interface.
   The hierarchy is read from left to right: fixed structural exponents and the terminal positive
gain are chosen before the small factoring, grid, shading, and discretization exponents. The
notation below records logical roles, not a new optimization of the source parameters.
   For comparison with the source notation, the complete interface chain is
             fixed (94) −→ global fine level −→ joint cellular factoring −→ (95–97)new
              −→ resolved ANGLE-CELL −→ one LIFT and d∠ −→ (98–103)new .
The chain terminates at rectangular carriers. Its sharp-parent extension is the open problem
isolated in section 12.
